So, it's often called slightly different things in different states. But misrepresentation or innocent misrepresentation is where there's two parties about to enter into a contract and one party knows something material to the transaction that they know the other side would like to know but chooses not to tell them. So when you're sitting in an office and you're about to buy a piece of property, you're the buyer, they're the seller. If they knew and didn't disclose that, that could be common law misrepresentation. However, however, you need to understand that misrepresentation has got to be about a fact that is material to the transaction. Now, that last guy I quoted said, "My wife says she wouldn't have bought here." Okay, if she had known that, she wouldn't have bought. But a lot of people look at they go away, 56 cents a trip, that's material to the transaction of a purchase of a house. In other words, I forget something, but it benefits me that I forgot it. So, you know, you know how that goes. And it reminds me of a book that I wrote called American Murder Houses. Somebody got somebody got hurt in this house?" [snorts] There have been examples where there was a house and and something horrific happened there and it got cleaned up and the people sold and somebody else moved in and bought it. And there's been litigation on this exact topic when it comes to if you bought a house from somebody and they failed to disclose that something really bad had happened there, could you try to unwind as a deal? And there's case law on it, I found, in at least two states, New York and California. And the interesting thing is that some states have now even passed laws about what you need to disclose above and beyond simply the things you disclose in any transaction. And so in some states, for instance, if the house was used to commit certain kinds of crimes where you manufacture things such as Breaking Bad, you may have to disclose the Breaking Bad history of your house. But the interesting thing was that a judge wrote, I believe it was a California case, a judge wrote, one of the most fascinating things about the concept of murder houses, if you study them, and the judge apparently did, is how often it is that someone can come and look at a house, walk around the house, maybe come back, take a second look at it, talk to their real estate agent who talks the other side's real estate agent, they negotiate a deal, handshakes, you go to a close, Everybody smiles and happy and they move out and you move in. The judge said, "The most amazing thing is how quickly the neighbors come running over to say, do you know what happened in your house?" [laughter] Contrast that with how you went through all the transaction of the buying process and the closing and the sellers forgot to mention it to you. And that was the argument in those cases. That was misrepresentation. Silent or innocent, but misrepresentation nonetheless, where they failed to disclose something that we would have liked to know and they did it to their benefit and our detriment. Now the most interesting thing I discovered was when I was researching my book is I found there are real estate people who studied this and the question is does it really affect the home value of a typical home where something happened such as what I mentioned earlier um but it's not the kind where there's a constant flow of traffic in front of the house. So the original file might contain the original documents and then copies go to the parties and so if I'm a party to a case it's been settled and a dismissal was entered, I might have a true copy of a dismissal and it'll I'll look at it. it'll be identical to the one that's in the court file and it has the same effect because it's it's a dismissal. Now, it is true that a lot of things have gone online now. So at subsection 2304 it says federal minimum standard for warranties remedies under written warranty. Again remedies under written warranty in order for a warrtor warranting a consumer product by means of a written warranty to meet the federal minimum standards for warranty. Such warrant must as a minimum remedy such consumer product within a reasonable time and without charge in the case a defect, malfunction or failure to conform with such written warranty. So the first thing you must do is you must be willing to remedy the problem. Again the three Rs the guy talked about number one remedy. So you must as a minimum remedy the product. Okay. Then sub4 says if the product contains a defect or malfunction after a reasonable number of attempts by the warranttor to remedy defects or malfunctions in such product, such warrant must permit the consumer to elect either a refund for or replacement without charge of such product. And now you say, "I would like to sue them for this." How many times did they try to repair it? Four. Is that a reasonable number of repair attempts? And I'm here to tell you right now that one of the reasons that a lot of attorneys don't like these cases is that the lemon law, for example, actually says how many times is reasonable.
